🟢 **2026-09-05 — ISHKHAN'S 2024 FORM 7203 IS IN HAND, AND IT CHANGES WHAT HIS 1040 PREPARER MUST BE TOLD** *(§3P-2)*:
- ✅ **His opening stock basis IS ZERO** — the paper's earlier doubt is withdrawn, so the 9,493.32 gain is not contingent.
- 🔴 **He carries 48,125 of §1366(d) SUSPENDED LOSSES from 2024** *(37,688 ordinary + 10,437 §1231)* on top of the 2025 loss — **total 50,795.73, and ALL of it dies on his final K-1**, because his basis is exhausted by the distributions on either presentation.
- 🛑 **HIS FORM 7203 IS PREPARED GROSS: line 2 = 50,538.70 contributions, line 6 = 60,032.02 distributions.** ⛔ **Box 16D reads 0 and that is presentation** — a preparer who takes the zero from the K-1 and the contributions from the client's records deducts **47,003.13 that the facts do not support** (§3P-2 ④.1).
- ⚠️ **His §199A carryforward, recorded below and in §7 as (68,058)/(18,847), is the GROSS 2024 loss and is probably OVERSTATED** — only 38,780 was allowed in 2024. 🛠️ **His 2024 Form 8995 is the missing half; ask for it.**
- ⛔ **The COMPENSATION line below is superseded: none of it is Ishkhan's** (§4 decision 24) — **all 18,986.63 is Artem's.**


🛑 **This is the highest-consequence thing on the return and NOTHING flags it.** ATX will file a
perfectly clean 1120-S while a misstatement lands on **somebody else's** tax return, discovered years
later as an IRS notice. 🔴 **With two shareholders it is now TWO separate computations that do not
inform each other** — one shareholder can have a taxable distribution while the other has room to
spare, and the entity return looks identical either way.

```
Total distributed to the owners in 2025 .................. 120,064.03
   → Ishkhan Markarian, 50% .............................. 60,032.02
   → Artem Markarian,  50% ............................... 60,032.01
(entity level: the AAA available to absorb them ......... nil - it is negative)
```

**Each shareholder's own stock basis, in the order §1367 and the Form 7203 instructions require:**

| # | Step | **Ishkhan** | **Artem** |
|---|---|---:|---:|
| 0 | Stock basis at 1/1/2025 | ⚠️ **his 2024 Form 7203 line 15** — not established; **0 assumed as the worst case** | 🔴 **CREATED when he was admitted** — not established; **0 assumed** |
| 1 | **+ capital contributions** ⚠️ **also an assumption** | **50,538.70** | **50,538.70** |
| 2 | **+ income items** — ordinary income | **20,172.59** | **20,172.59** |
| | *subtotal before distributions* | **70,711.29** | **70,711.29** |
| 3 | **− distributions** | **(60,032.02)** | **(60,032.01)** |
| | ✅ *room left* | **10,679.27** | **10,679.28** |
| 4 | **− nondeductible expenses** — the meals | **(3,535.57)** | **(3,535.57)** |
| 5 | **− losses and deductions** — *any §179 lands HERE* | **0** | **0** |
| | 🔑 **Ending stock basis** | **7,143.70** | **7,143.71** |

🛑 **THE TABLE ABOVE IS AT DEPRECIATION = 0, AND ITS ANSWER NO LONGER HOLDS ONCE §2D IS POSTED.**
⛔ **Read this row pair together:**

| At | Income at step 2, each | Room after distributions | Verdict |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| **D = 0** *(the table above)* | 20,172.59 | **+10,679.27** | ✅ **both fit — no capital gain** |
| 🔴 **D = 26,700** *(§2D, the election ATX carries)* | **6,822.59** | 🔴 **−2,670.73** | 🔴 **BOTH have a capital gain — 2,670.73 and 2,670.72.** Ending stock basis is **ZERO for each**, and the 3,535.57 of nondeductibles at step 4 has no basis left to absorb it |

⚖️ **The entity-level threshold is unchanged at 21,358.55** — halving every input halves the room too,
so a depreciation deduction turns each of them negative above roughly that figure *(each share falls by
`D ÷ 2` against room of 10,679.27)*. **26,700 is above it, which is exactly why the gain appears.**
🟢 **Taking the deduction is still the right trade** — §3H-2 prices it — **but the gain is real, it is
reported on Form 7203 and Schedule D of each 1040, and NOTHING on this 1120-S will mention it.**

🛑 **THE ORDER IS NOT NEGOTIABLE AND IT IS EASY TO GET WRONG.** Income first, **then distributions**,
then nondeductibles, **then losses and deductions.** ⚠️ **Distributions come BEFORE losses** — an
earlier version of this paper had them the other way round, which would have freed a suspended loss
the basis cannot support. The §1.1367-1(g) election moves losses ahead of **nondeductibles only** —
never ahead of distributions.

#### 🔴 WHAT BREAKS THIS, AND THE FIRST ONE IS THE MOST LIKELY

**1 · 🛑 THE SPLIT TURNING OUT NOT TO BE 50/50 — and BOTH halves of it are ASSUMPTIONS, not findings.**
⚠️ **Step 1 as well as step 3.** The 50 contribution rows are pooled in one account exactly as the 94
distribution rows are, and **step 1 moves the room dollar for dollar** — so it decides the answer as
much as step 3 does.

🔴 **AND HERE IS THE NUMBER THAT MATTERS MOST, WHICH AN EARLIER VERSION OF THIS SECTION DID NOT WORK.**
On the 50/50 **contribution** assumption, each shareholder has **70,711.29** of basis before
distributions. **So a shareholder whose ACTUAL distributions exceed 70,711.29 has a capital gain with
NO depreciation at all** — and 70,711.29 is **58.9%** of the 120,064.03 distributed.
🛑 **A 60/40 split already produces one.** If the contributions were largely Ishkhan's while the
distributions ran substantially to Artem, **Artem's gain reaches five figures.**
⛔ **So "the split decides how much depreciation is safe" is TRUE BUT INCOMPLETE — the split can create
tax on its own, with no election made.** *(An earlier version worked only the favourable extreme — all
contributions and all distributions Ishkhan's, room down to about 1,186, still no gain without
depreciation — and presented that as the size of the exposure. It is the smaller half of it.)*
✅ **Both are illustrations of the mechanism, not claims about the facts. Both are why the real split
has to be established.**

**2 · The opening basis of either of them.** ~~Ishkhan's is his 2024 Form 7203 line 15 — it exists and is
**not zero**, so the table above understates him.~~ 🟢 **CORRECTED 2026-09-05 — THE FORM ARRIVED AND
LINE 15 IS ZERO** *(§3P-2)*. **The table above does not understate him; the zero is real, and his gain
stands.** 🔴 **What the same form DID carry is 48,125 of §1366(d) SUSPENDED LOSSES into 2025**, which no
table here knew about and which die on his final K-1. **Artem's is CREATED at his admission** and its size
depends on how: **what he paid** on a purchase; **his father's basis carried over under §1015** on a
gift; **the dual-basis rule of Reg. §1.1015-4** on a bargain sale, *which §B-2 calls the common case
between family members*; **what he paid for new shares** on an issuance. ⛔ **Nothing in this engagement
has established which**, and it is the same open item as §3S row 7.

**3 · A 2024 suspended loss of Ishkhan's — 🟢 CONFIRMED 2026-09-05: IT IS 48,125** *(37,688 ordinary +
10,437 §1231 — §3P-2 ①)*, and the rest of this paragraph was written before the form arrived. 2024 was a **(89,292.05)** book-loss year and he owned all
of it. If his basis could not absorb it, the excess is suspended under **§1366(d)(2)** and is a step-5
item here — it would consume the 7,143.70 and, on a **final** K-1, be **lost**. ~~⛔ **The figure is in his 2024 Form 7203 Part III**, on his own return.~~ ✅ **It is, and it is now in
hand.**

**4 · A depreciation election.** §3H, and note it splits: **each shareholder absorbs half.**

⛔ **"An opening basis below zero" is NOT a failure mode** — stock basis cannot go negative under
§1367(a)(2), which is precisely why "assume zero" is the valid worst case at step 0.

#### 🛠️ HOW THE REAL SPLIT GETS DETERMINED — the method, for when Julia calls it

**The ledger holds 94 distribution rows and 50 contribution rows for 2025, and it does not say whose
each one is.** ⛔ **It cannot be derived by arithmetic — it is read row by row.**

| # | Pass | What it settles |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | **The rows that carry a NAME settle themselves** | the Zelle payments name their recipient; the Hyundai Motor Finance ACHs carry **two different names** |
| **2** | **Rows that pay an identifiable obligation** | the monthly apartment rent *(whose apartment?)*, the health-insurance premiums *(whose policy?)* |
| **3** | **Card spending** | **whose card is each one** |
| **4** | **Cash withdrawals** | one of them already names Artem |
| **5** | 🔑 **The two personal checking accounts** money leaves from and returns to | ⛔ **the ledger cannot answer this and it is the biggest single lever — ASK.** Whose accounts are they? |
| **6** | **What is left after 1–5** | goes to Julia and the client as a named list, in the §D-9 register |

🛠️ **AND THE BOOKS NEED SOMEWHERE TO PUT THE ANSWER.** Today there is **one** contribution account and
**one** distribution account for two people. The structural fix is **a sub-account per shareholder**:

```
Owner's contribution            (parent - left empty from here on)
├── Owner's contribution - Ishkhan Markarian
└── Owner's contribution - Artem Markarian
Owner's distribution            (parent - left empty from here on)
├── Owner's distribution - Ishkhan Markarian
└── Owner's distribution - Artem Markarian
```

⛔ **NOT the `NAME` column on a journal entry.** In QuickBooks that column takes a **Customer, Vendor or
Employee** — there is no shareholder type — so tagging a shareholder there files him as a **VENDOR**,
which feeds the **1099 workflow this return already has an open question about** (§3I). **It also
produces no balance**: you cannot read "distributions by shareholder" off the balance sheet from it.
✅ **Sub-accounts show each owner's total as a line, and each Form 7203 then reads off a line instead of
a query.**

🔗 **Form 7203 is NOT filed with this return. There are now TWO of them**, one on each shareholder's own
Form 1040, and the table above is exactly what each needs. ⛔ **Preparing either 1040 is a separate
request on a separate day** *(§8)*.

#### ② 🛑 TWO THINGS THIS PAPER HAD WRONG, and one of them was load-bearing

| What the paper said | 🔴 **What the document shows** |
|---|---|
| *"his 2024 Form 7203 line 15 exists and **is not zero**"* — used in §3H-4 ④, §3P and §3S row 0a to make the capital gain **contingent** | ⛔ **A GUESS, AND WRONG. Line 15 IS ZERO.** ✅ **The zero-opening-basis assumption every table was built on is CORRECT** — and the gain is not contingent on it any more |
| His §199A carryforward recorded as **(68,058)** ordinary and **(18,847)** §1231 *(§7, §3N, §3O)* | ⚠️ **Those are the GROSS 2024 losses; only 38,780 was allowed.** A loss blocked by basis does not enter QBI until it is allowed, so **the §199A figure this paper carries is probably overstated.** 🛠️ **Verify it against his 2024 Form 8995, not against the 7203** — they are different mechanisms that happen to start from the same two numbers |

#### ③ 🔴 WHAT HAPPENS TO HIS BASIS IN 2025 — and the answer does NOT depend on the netting

🛑 **AN EARLIER VERSION OF THIS BLOCK, WRITTEN AND DELIVERED THE SAME DAY, GAVE FOUR "SCENARIOS" AND
RECOMMENDED ONE OF THEM. IT WAS WRONG, AND IT WAS PUT TO LILIAN AS A RECOMMENDATION ABOUT A REAL
PERSON'S RETURN.** ⛔ **The error, caught in review: it took the NETTED distribution and the GROSS
contribution at the same time — using the same 50,538.70 twice, once to raise his basis and once to
reduce his distributions.** 🔑 **Netting is a wash. Applied consistently it changes nothing, which is
what [`form-1120s-preparation.md`](../../sops/form-1120s-preparation.md) §5C-v says under *"why it is
arithmetically neutral"*.** The corrected analysis is below.

**His 2025 inputs (the 50/50 assumption, gross):** opening basis **0** · contributions **50,538.70** ·
distributions **60,032.02** · nondeductibles **3,535.57** · 2025 loss **2,670.73** · **carryover 48,125**
→ **total losses available 50,795.73.** **Order:** contributions → distributions → nondeductibles →
losses *(item ① row E)*.

| 7203 line | ✅ **GROSS — the law** | ✅ **NETTED, applied CONSISTENTLY** | ⛔ **THE HYBRID — what a preparer will do by accident** |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| 2 · contributions | 50,538.70 | 🔑 **0** *(netted away)* | 50,538.70 |
| 5 · basis before distributions | 50,538.70 | **0** | 50,538.70 |
| 6 · distributions | 60,032.02 | 🔑 **9,493.32** *(the un-netted excess)* | ⛔ **0** *(read straight off box 16D)* |
| 🔴 **CAPITAL GAIN** | 🔴 **9,493.32** | 🔴 **9,493.32** | **0** |
| 7 · basis after distributions | 0 | 0 | 50,538.70 |
| 8a · nondeductibles | *no basis* | *no basis* | (3,535.57) |
| 10 · basis before losses | **0** | **0** | 47,003.13 |
| **11 · losses deductible on his 1040** | **0** | **0** | ⛔ **47,003.13** |
| 15 · ending basis | 0 | 0 | 0 |
| 🛑 **Losses that DIE on his final K-1** | 🔴 **50,795.73** | 🔴 **50,795.73** | 3,792.60 |

🔑 **THE TWO CORRECT COLUMNS ARE THE SAME COLUMN.** ✅ **So the netting decision does not change
Ishkhan's tax at all**, and §3H-4 ④ was right the first time: *"it does NOT remove Ishkhan's capital
gain."* 🛑 **His answer is a 9,493.32 capital gain, zero ending basis, and 50,795.73 of losses lost.**

⛔ **AND §5C-v DOES NOT GIVE HIM ANYTHING ELSE.** Its **gate 1 FAILS for him** — his distributions
exceed his contributions — and a failed gate means **gross**, which is the first column. **Its
gates-fail route** *(the block this firm added on 2026-09-05)* books the excess as **officer
compensation** and then reports the remainder at zero — ⛔ **but §4 decision 24 already gave the whole
of that compensation to ARTEM**, so on this return there is nothing of it for Ishkhan.
🛑 **§3H-4 ③'s middle row is therefore MISLABELLED and is corrected there** — `9,493.32 / 9,493.32 / 0`
is *"each account netted against itself"*, **not** *"§5C-v applied per shareholder"*, which would give
`60,032.02 / 60,032.02 / 0`.

#### ④ 🛑 THE REAL RISK, AND THE ONLY THING THAT WOULD ACTUALLY CHANGE HIS ANSWER

**1 · 🔴 THE HYBRID COLUMN IS THE FAILURE MODE, AND IT IS THE EASY MISTAKE, NOT THE EXOTIC ONE.**
A preparer opens his K-1, reads **box 16D = 0**, takes his contributions from the client's records
because they are real, and produces **47,003.13 of deduction** on his 2025 Form 1040. ⛔ **Nothing on
the K-1 stops it**, and it is wrong for a reason that is invisible from the K-1: the zero is a
presentation choice made on a different return, and **the contributions were netted away to produce
it.** 🛠️ **THE FIX: whoever prepares his 1040 is told, in writing, that box 16D reads zero by
presentation and his GROSS distributions are 60,032.02** — §3S row 0c, and §8C.

**2 · 🛑 WHAT ACTUALLY DECIDES HIS RETURN IS A FACT, NOT A PRESENTATION — THE SPLIT OF THE POOLED
ACCOUNT.** Everything above rests on 50/50, which §3P says is Lilian's working assumption and not a
finding. 🔑 **If his real distributions are at or below his real contributions, he has no gain AND the
suspended losses become deductible up to his basis** — the difference between a 9,493.32 gain and a
five-figure deduction. ⚠️ **The 48,125 raises the stakes of the split by roughly six times, and the
split is fact-finding, which is why it belongs in front of Julia now rather than at the 1040.**

**3 · 🔴 THE 48,125 DIES IN HIS FINAL YEAR AND ALMOST NOTHING CAN SAVE IT.** ⛔ **§1366(d)(3)'s
post-termination transition period does not reach him** — it runs when the CORPORATION's S election
terminates, not when one shareholder disposes of his stock. ⓘ **The one statutory carve-out,
§1366(d)(2)(B), is a transfer to a spouse or former spouse incident to divorce** — not a father-to-son
transfer, so it does not apply here, but the rule is not the absolute the first draft made it. **2025
is closed, so nothing can be added to his basis now.** 🛠️ **VERIFY BOTH AGAINST THE CURRENT
INSTRUCTIONS BEFORE THIS REACHES HIS 1040** — this session could not reach irs.gov, and the figure it
governs is 50,795.73.

**4 · ⚠️ THE ORDERING ELECTION — flagged, not closed.** Item ① reads the unticked Reg. §1.1367-1(g)
box off the **2024** form, which establishes **2024's** ordering. 🔑 **The election is the
SHAREHOLDER's, made on his own return**, so 2024 does not settle 2025 by itself. ⓘ **On the correct
answer it is worth NOTHING** — his basis is zero after distributions, so neither nondeductibles nor
losses can be absorbed in either order. ⚠️ **It is only worth the 3,535.57 of nondeductibles if the
split changes and he ends up with basis to allocate** *(losses-first would deduct 50,538.70 instead of
47,003.13)*. 🛠️ **Read the current instructions before making or assuming it.**

**5 · ⚠️ "LOSSES DEDUCTIBLE" IS NOT ONE LINE ON HIS 1040, if the split ever gives him basis.** The
50,795.73 is **40,358.73 ordinary** *(2,670.73 + 37,688)* and **10,437 §1231**, and where basis is
short the allowance is **prorated across the categories** — it is not a single figure to type. ⓘ *On
the correct answer this is moot: nothing is allowed.*

📄 **And the SHAREHOLDER's instructions say what he does with it:** *"Code AC. Gross receipts for
section 448(c). **Use the gross receipts amount to figure the business interest expense you can
deduct**, if applicable. See section 163(j) **and the Instructions for Form 8990** for details."* 🔑 **So the figure feeds his own §163(j) small-business
exemption test.**

##### 🔎 WHERE 13,005 COMES FROM — searched, and the answer is "not from this return"

**Lilian asked. Three searches were run and all three come back negative:**

| Search | Result |
|---|---|

carrying it from an INPUT**, either typed into the §448(c)/§163(j) gross-receipts field or rolled over
from the prior-year ATX file. **That is why Lilian can overtype it, and why nothing on the return
explains it.**

refund**. ⛔ **It cannot be the source.** That number was computed in THIS PAPER; it is not in

match is to the cent and it is still a coincidence** — do not "rediscover" it and write it up as the

🛑 **THIS BLOCK WAS MARKED "WRONG YEAR" ON 2026-09-05 AND THAT MARKING WAS ITSELF THE ERROR.** ⛔ **The
figure here was correct from the start; it was un-marked on 2026-09-06 after Lilian's unchecking test —
§3T-4.** 🔑 **Only the KEYING ROUTE below was ever wrong: it goes in ONE entity field on the
`Ln 17d, Sch K - Oth Items` worksheet, and ATX halves it to the K-1s at 134,951 / 134,950 — never as two
per-K-1 overrides.** 📌 **The lesson is in §3T-4's last block: a superseding note is a change to a signed
figure and deserves the same evidence as the figure did.**

| Source | Amount |
|---|---:|
| Page 1 line 1c — gross receipts or sales | **269,901** |
| Page 1 line 4 — Form 4797 · line 5 — other income | 0 |
| Schedule K — interest · dividends · rents · royalties · capital gains | 0 |
| ✅ **2025 gross receipts for §448(c) purposes** *(the current-year row — **not** code AC)* | ✅ **269,901** |

🛑 **CORRECTED 2026-09-05 — AN EARLIER VERSION OF THIS BLOCK SAID TO PUT 269,901 ON BOTH K-1s. THAT
WAS WRONG, AND LILIAN CAUGHT IT.** ⛔ **She asked whether it should not be half, since box 17 exists only
on each shareholder's own K-1. It should. Her reasoning was right and mine was not.**

📄 **The 2025 Instructions for Form 8990, pulled from irs.gov, say it in terms:**

> *"For section 163(j), a taxpayer with an ownership interest in a partnership or S corporation must
> include a share of the partnership's or S corporation's gross receipts, in proportion to the partner's
> distributive share of items of gross income or **S corporation's shareholder's pro rata share of gross
> receipts**."*

🔑 **So each K-1 carries THAT shareholder's share** *(⚠️ a convention, not a quoted rule — see §11F ④ of
the SOP for the contrary reading the sources leave open)*:

⛔ **ROUTE A WAS CHOSEN — §4 decision 29. NOTHING IN THIS BLOCK IS KEYED INTO THE 2025 FILING.**

| K-1 · box 17 code AC | **What ATX would PRINT under route B** — ⛔ **not a keying instruction** | ƒ |
|---|---:|---|
| **Ishkhan** | **134,950** | ƒ `269,901 × 50%`, rounded |
| **Artem** | **134,951** | ƒ the other half — **the two must sum to 269,901** |

🛑 **Under route B this is what the software produces from ONE entity field. It is never typed on the
K-1s** — a per-K-1 override is exactly what decision 29 orders removed.

⛔ **THE REASONING THAT LED ME WRONG, recorded so it is not repeated:** I argued *"ATX shows the same
figure on both K-1s, therefore the field is entity-level."* 🛑 **That inference runs backwards.** The
identical figure is **evidence the field is misconfigured**, not evidence of what it is for. **A K-1 is a
per-shareholder form; box 17 is a per-shareholder box.** ✅ **Lilian reasoned from what the form IS; I
reasoned from what the software happened to print.**

#### 🔴 F3 · **FORM 4562 PART V — TWO SIGNED "YES" ANSWERS SITTING ON TOP OF ZERO MILES**

| Line | What the return says |
|---|---|
| **24a** *"Do you have evidence to support the business/investment use claimed?"* | ✅ **Yes** |
| **24b** *"If 'Yes,' is the evidence written?"* | ✅ **Yes** |
| Business use, both vehicles | **100.00%** |
| 🔴 **Section B — miles driven** | **business 0 · commuting 0 · other 0 · TOTAL 0** |

🛑 **A vehicle with zero total miles cannot have 100% business use — the same form contradicts itself**,
and §3S item 4 records that **no mileage log has ever been seen for this client.** These are two
assertions of fact on a signed return.

##### 🔴 THE 2024 RETURN, RE-READ 2026-09-05 ON LILIAN'S ASK — and it changes the argument

**Read off the filed 2024 return in Double, every amount mapped to its line by coordinate.**

| | **2024** | **2025** |
|---|---|---|
| **The vehicle(s)** | 🔑 **`2022 RAM 2500` — a heavy-duty PICKUP** | `2014 Isuzu NPR` **box truck** + `2017 RAM ProMaster 3500` **cargo van** |
| **Is it listed property?** | ✅ **YES, and correctly so** — the Form 4562 instructions name **"pickup trucks"** in the very list of property whose *"nature lends itself to personal use"* | ⚠️ **probably NOT** — a commercial truck and a cargo van are the exception's own examples |
| Business-use % declared | **100.00%** | **100.00%** |
| Business miles | **0** | **0** |
| Commuting miles | **0** | **0** |
| **Personal miles** | 🔴 **5,407** | **0** |
| Total miles | **5,407** | **0** |
| **24a — evidence exists?** | **Yes** | **Yes** |
| 🔴 **24b — is it WRITTEN?** | 🔴 **NO** | 🔴 **YES** |
| **§179** *(line 29)* | **0** | **0** |
| 🔴 **Bonus / special allowance** *(line 25)* | 🔴 **BLANK — none taken** | 🔴 **23,000** |
| Depreciation claimed | **12,071** — plain 200DB MACRS | **26,700** — 100% bonus |

**🛑 FOUR THINGS THIS SETTLES, and three of them cut against the 2025 return as keyed:**

**① 2024 carried the SAME internal contradiction, and worse.** A vehicle at **100.00% business use** whose
own Section B says **zero business miles and 5,407 personal ones.** ⛔ **That does not just fail to
support the percentage — it contradicts it outright.** ✅ **Corroborated twice:** Form 4562 Section B and
the separate `Use of Vehicles` statement page agree.

**② BUT 2024 ANSWERED 24b HONESTLY: `No`, the evidence is not written.** 🔴 **The 2025 return answers
`Yes`.** **Same client, same absent log, opposite answer** — a change of position with nothing new
behind it. 🛠️ **If the two vehicles stay in Part V, `No` is the answer the client's own filing history
supports.**

**③ 🔴 THE ONE THAT MOVES MONEY: 2024 TOOK NO BONUS AND NO §179 ON THE VEHICLE.** Line 25 blank, line 29
zero, plain MACRS. 🔑 **Read line 25's own title — *"Special depreciation allowance for qualified listed
property placed in service during the tax year AND USED MORE THAN 50% IN A QUALIFIED BUSINESS USE."***
**At 0 business miles the 2024 vehicle could not pass that test, and the return did not claim it.**
⛔ **The 2025 return claims 23,000 under that heading on a mileage record that is emptier still.** ⚖️ **So
the prior year is not a precedent for the 2025 treatment — it is the opposite of one.**

**④ ✅ The disposal ties out completely.** `2022 RAM 2500`, acquired 1/30/2023, **sold 17/03/2024** —
gross sales 45,600, depreciation allowed 31,200, cost 95,647 → **§1231 loss (18,847)** on Form 4797 line 7.
`45,600 + 31,200 − 95,647 = (18,847)` ✅ **exact**, and it is the same 18,847 that appears on **Ishkhan's
2024 Form 7203 Part III** *(8,410 allowed, **10,437 carried into 2025**)* — §3P-2 ①. **Everything ties.**

**🔑 AND THE CONCLUSION FOR THE 2025 DECISION, which is the point of looking:**
⛔ **"We did it this way last year" is NOT an argument here.** **Last year's vehicle was a pickup — genuinely
listed property, correctly in Part V.** **This year's two are a box truck and a cargo van, which is a
different question entirely.** ✅ **ATX put them in Part V because the asset code is `V-7`, not because
anyone tested them** — and the prior year cannot be cited to justify keeping them there.

🛠️ **THE CLEAN FIX:** a medium-duty truck and a cargo van used only in the business are very likely
**qualified nonpersonal use vehicles** *(Reg. §1.274-5T(k))*, which are **not listed property at all** —
take them out of Part V and the mileage question disappears. **Test it and record the conclusion.** ⛔ **If
they stay in Part V, either the real mileage goes in or 24a/24b are answered honestly.**
✅ **The 26,700 and the 100% bonus do not change either way** — neither vehicle is near the §280F
passenger-automobile caps by weight. **What is at stake is substantiation, not the deduction.**

> paper is GONE** — it returned 403 through the proxy on 2026-09-04 and returns **200** on 2026-09-06.

> 🛠️ **Anything still unanswered about Connecticut can now be READ, and must be.**
>
> 📄 **Sources, downloaded this session:** `2025 Form CT-1065/CT-1120SI` *(8 pages)* and its
> **Instructions (Rev. 12/25, 14 pages)**, plus the DRS **Pass-Through Entity Tax** page. Every quotation
> below is verbatim from them.

#### 🛑 THREE THINGS THIS PAPER GOT WRONG, AND ONE OF THEM WAS ACTED ON

| | What the paper said | 📄 What the instructions say |
|---|---|---|
| 🔴 **CT1** | *"Part 3 `Place(s) of Business` is COMPLETELY BLANK … **Fill it**"* — **said twice, and Lilian filled it in** | ⛔ **WRONG. Part 3 is LEFT BLANK for this entity.** Instructions **p.7** *(the page whose own footer reads "Page 7 of 14" — a concatenated extract puts the marker a page late)*: *"Complete Part 3 only if the PE carries on business both within and outside Connecticut."* **A blank Part 3 was CORRECT all along, and the "contradiction" this paper saw was the opposite of a defect.** ⚠️ **TWO HONEST QUALIFICATIONS BELOW — the premise is the return's own assertion, and the 403 did not cause this one** |
| 🔴 **CT4-b** | *"a per-member, per-month late-filing penalty — historically $50 per member per month, capped at five months"* | ⛔ **WRONG, and far smaller.** *"The penalty for late payment of the tax due is 10% of the amount due. In the event that no tax is due, the Commissioner of Revenue Services may impose a $50 penalty for the late filing of any return or report required by law to be filed."* — **emphasis added below, not inside the quote** 🔑 **$50, discretionary, per return.** ⓘ *The per-member-per-month shape is the FEDERAL late-filing penalty for a 1065/1120-S; it was imported without checking* |
| ⚠️ **CT5** | *"the election box on the face of the form … tick it or not"* | 🟡 **HALF RIGHT — and a first version of this row got the other half BACKWARDS. See the correction directly below** |

##### 🛑 CT5 · **THE CORRECTION TO THE CORRECTION — the box IS the election, and it has a DEADLINE**

⛔ **A 2026-09-06 version of this section said: *"The box is not the election — it is a statement that a
separate return was filed."*** 🛑 **That is WRONG, and the page this very section quotes for "irrevocable"
says so in the next sentence.**

📄 **DRS, Composite Income Tax Information, verbatim:**

> *"The entity may elect to file Form CT-PET, Connecticut Pass-Through Entity Tax Return, in addition to
> Form CT-1065/CT-1120SI, Connecticut Composite Income Tax Return, **by checking the box at the bottom of
> the first page of Form CT-1065/CT-1120SI and filing Form CT-PET. The election is irrevocable and cannot
> be amended. If the PE does not elect to file Form CT-PET on their Form CT-1065/CT1120SI, the PE cannot
> elect in on an amended return.**"*

📄 **And the DRS Pass-Through Entity page:** *"To make the election, entities must provide written notice
to the Commissioner of Revenue Services no later than the due date (or extended due date) of the return.
**Checking off the box on a timely filed Form CT-1065/CT-1120SI … will constitute written notice.**"*

| 🔑 What is actually true | |
|---|---|
| **The election is made by TWO acts together** | **checking the box** on Form CT-1065/CT-1120SI **and filing Form CT-PET** |
| ✅ **The half the first correction got right** | electing does oblige a **whole second return** — it is not "just a checkbox" |
| 🔴 **The half it got WRONG, and this is the sharp end** | the box **is** the election, and it must be on a **TIMELY FILED ORIGINAL return.** ⛔ **"If the PE does not elect … the PE cannot elect in on an amended return."** 🛑 **There is no second chance. Transmitting without the box CLOSES the 2025 election permanently** |
| ⚖️ **What that does to CT5's status** | **it makes the deadline the decision.** Julia's answer is needed **before transmission**, not before some later amendment — which is what §CT-FIX CT5 said and this section briefly undermined |

⛔ **The instruction sentence that produced the error — *"Check the Pass-Through Entity Tax box if you have
filed 2025 Form CT-PET"* — is verbatim, and reading it alone inverts it.** 📌 **The lesson is the same one
§11F was written about: a quotation that settles a question needs the sentences around it.**

⚖️ **AND ONE THING THE PAPER GUESSED AND GOT RIGHT:** the PE tax **is** optional and the election **is**
irrevocable. 📄 **DRS, verbatim:** *"The Pass-Through Entity (PE) Tax is optional. Entities that elect
to pay the PE Tax must do so annually, and the election is irrevocable."*

##### ⚠️ CT1 · **TWO QUALIFICATIONS, because the reversal is right and its ARGUMENT was not**

| | |
|---|---|
| ⚠️ **The premise is CIRCULAR as stated** | this section proved "100% Connecticut" with **Part 2 line 8 = 1.000000** — ⛔ **which is what the preparer typed, not an independent fact.** 📄 The form's own note: *"If the PE carries on business **only within** Connecticut, enter 1.000000 (100%) on Line 8 and leave the other lines blank."* 🔴 **If any 2025 receipts were sourced outside Connecticut, Part 3 becomes REQUIRED and the fraction on line 8 is wrong.** 🛠️ **What settles it: were any 2025 jobs performed outside Connecticut?** ⚠️ **This paper spends pages on Florida activity** *(§D-7, the vehicle and fuel analysis)* — **which was read as the OWNER's travel, not the company's work.** ✅ **If that reading holds, 1.000000 is right.** **It has never been put as a question, and it should be** |
| ⛔ **The "portal was blocked" story is FALSE for this one** | **the disqualifying sentence is printed ON THE FORM ITSELF**, directly under the Part 3 heading: *"Complete only if the PE carries on business both within and outside Connecticut."* 🛑 **§CT-FIX said its instructions were "read off the form's own face" — and this sentence is on that face.** ⛔ **So CT1 was not caused by portal.ct.gov returning 403. It was a line on the page that was not read** *(or an ATX facsimile that omits it — worth checking, and worth recording either way)*. 📌 **The transferable lesson is not "get the portal unblocked": it is RE-READ A SCHEDULE'S OWN GATING LINE BEFORE CALLING A BLANK A DEFECT** |

#### 🗺️ WHICH PARTS THIS ENTITY MUST COMPLETE — the whole map, from the instructions

**Everything turns on ONE fact: both members are `RI` — resident individuals.** 📄 **The official member
type codes:** `NI` nonresident/part-year individual · `NT` nonresident/part-year trust · `NE` nonresident
estate · `PE` pass-through entity · **`RI` resident individual** · `RT` resident trust · `RE` resident
estate · `CM` corporate member.

#### 🔴 F6 · **FORM 8990 IS ATTACHED TO THE RETURN, AND IT SHOULD NOT BE** — *a FEDERAL finding, recorded here because it surfaced in the same read; it belongs to the §3T review series*

**Pages 13–15 of the generated package carry a Form 8990, entirely zeros** *(line 21 total `0`, line 22
adjusted taxable income `0`)*. ⛔ **ATX's own review list says it:** *"8990 — Warning: If form 8990 is not
needed, please discard."*

🔑 **It is not needed — and "small business taxpayer" is NOT the whole reason.** 📄 **The operative
sentence is the EXCLUSION:** *"A taxpayer isn't required to file Form 8990 if the taxpayer is a small
business taxpayer **and doesn't have excess business interest expense from a partnership**."*
🛑 **So question 10 has to be right on ALL THREE of its legs, not just the receipts one** *(the firm-wide
check is [`form-1120s-preparation.md`](../../sops/form-1120s-preparation.md) §14)*:

| Leg | | This company |
|---|---|---|
| **(a)** | owns a pass-through with current or prior-year carryover **excess business interest expense** | ✅ **No** — ⚠️ **but read the evidence honestly.** Schedule B **question 4b tests 20% or more directly, or 50% or more directly-or-indirectly** — **a `No` there does NOT rule out a smaller interest, and EBIE is allocated from an interest of ANY size.** 🔑 **The load-bearing half is that no received K-1 appears in anything the firm holds on this client** — ⚠️ **and a received K-1 is not a ledger entry, so that is corroboration, not proof** |
| **(b)** | receipts over the threshold **with** business interest expense | ✅ **No** — 269,901 against $31,000,000 |
| **(c)** | a tax shelter **with** business interest expense | 🟡 **Reasoned, NOT verified.** §448(d)(3) points to §461(i)(3), which has **three** prongs: **(A)** a registered offering — ✅ none; **(B)** a **syndicate** under §1256(e)(3)(B) — **more than 35% of the year's losses allocable to limited partners or LIMITED ENTREPRENEURS**; **(C)** §6662(d)(2)(C)(ii). ⚠️ **Prong (B) is not tested by saying "no syndicate allocation".** 🔴 **2025 IS a loss year with 50% allocated to Ishkhan, and this paper nowhere establishes that he actively participates in management** — his §199A wage line was removed entirely, he exits at 12/31/2025, and §CT-FIX CT3 says his status is unverified. **A shareholder who does not actively participate is a limited entrepreneur.** 🛠️ **Either establish his participation — it is load-bearing elsewhere too — or carry this as reasoned, not verified** |

✅ **`No` is the right answer ON THE EVIDENCE — (b) is PROVED; (a) and (c) are REASONED, and nothing found contradicts either.** 🛑 **Not the same as "right on all three", and the difference matters because this sentence is what licenses removing the form.** ⚠️ **If Ishkhan's participation turns out to be passive, revisit leg (c) BEFORE filing.**

🛠️ **Remove it from the form list.** 📌 **This is [`tax-return-sop`](../../../.claude/skills/tax-return-sop/) §3A check 3 catching exactly what it
was written for:** *"tax software attaches a form the moment its parent line is touched, and fixing the
line does not detach the form."* **Line 13 interest expense was worked on repeatedly this week; the 8990
is the residue.** ⚠️ **A blank Form 8990 asserts that the corporation computed a §163(j) limitation it is
exempt from.**

##### 🧭 WHY IT MAY HAVE HAPPENED — hypotheses, best first, none of them an accusation

🛑 **This is work to be done, not an alarm** *([`method.md`](../../pre-return-review/method.md): a
mistake in the record is work).* **Recorded so the question put to Julia is a real question and not a
challenge.**

| | Hypothesis | Fits? |
|---|---|---|
| **①** | 🔴 **THE ELECTIVE-TAX TRAP. Connecticut's pass-through-entity TAX went from MANDATORY to OPTIONAL for tax years beginning on or after 1 Jan 2024** — and *"the CT PE tax is now optional"* reads almost identically to *"the CT PE filing is now optional"*. ⚠️ **The tax became elective; the RETURN did not** *(⛔ unverified — portal.ct.gov blocked)* | 🟢 **the best fit for 2024, and 2024 is EXACTLY the first year of the change.** ⛔ **It does not explain 2023** |
| **②** | **Nothing to pay.** All members resident, apportionment 100% CT, entity tax zero → a judgement that filing bought nothing | 🟡 coherent, and wrong if the return is required regardless |
| **③** | **It WAS filed — outside ATX, straight on myconneCT, and never saved back to Double** | 🟡 possible, **but the firm's own habit cuts against it**: the 2025 state extension *is* saved in Double |
| **④** | **2024 was the collapse year** — revenue fell to a fraction of 2023's. **A low-activity year is exactly when a state return gets dropped** | 🟡 plausible for 2024 alone |
| **⑤** | **It was simply missed**, and **CT DRS writing every six weeks since January 2026** is the state noticing something about this client | 🟡 the notices are about **withholding**, a different obligation — ⚠️ **but they establish that CT is actively looking at this entity** |

##### 🚨 WHY IT MATTERS FOR **THIS** RETURN, TODAY

1. 🔴 **`01012025` IS THE SYMPTOM, NOT A TYPO.** It is what the software writes when **there is no prior
   CT return to roll a date from.** ⛔ **Transmitted, it becomes a written statement to DRS that reads as
   the explanation for why 2023 and 2024 are missing** — from a company that has been in Connecticut
   since 2022.
2. 🟢 **CORRECTED 2026-09-06 FROM THE INSTRUCTIONS — the penalty is FAR smaller than this row claimed.**
   ~~*"historically $50 per member per month, capped at five months, i.e. per member, per year"*~~ ⛔ **That
   is the FEDERAL shape, imported without checking.** 📄 **Connecticut, verbatim:** *"In the event that no
   tax is due, the Commissioner of Revenue Services **may** impose a **$50 penalty** for the late filing of
   any return or report required by law to be filed."* 🔑 **$50, discretionary, per return.** ⚖️ **So the
   exposure on two missing years is small — but the OBLIGATION is unchanged, and an unfiled return does
   not start a statute of limitations.**
3. ✅ **Filing late voluntarily is a much smaller problem than being found** — and **DRS is already
   writing to this client.**

#### 🔑 WHY MOVING IT ALL TO ARTEM IS NOT THE FIX — and it is an understandable mistake

⚖️ **The change reads as a faithful application of §4 decision 24** *("the whole compensation is Artem's")*.
🛑 **But that decision is about WHOSE INCOME IT IS, and this field is about WHETHER IT WAS PAYROLL.** They
are two different questions and only the first has been decided.

🔑 **THE STRATEGY HAS THREE MOVING PARTS AND EACH LIVES IN A DIFFERENT PLACE:**

| The part | Where it is expressed | Status |
|---|---|---|
| **① It is compensation, not a distribution** | **1120-S page 1 line 7** | ✅ done — 18,987 |
| **② It is NOT payroll** | **line 8 blank · no W-2 · and NO §199A W-2 wages anywhere** | 🔴 **half done** — line 8 is blank, the wages field is not |
| **③ It is ARTEM's, and he pays the tax** | 🔑 **HIS Schedule C and Schedule SE, on his own 1040** | ⏭️ still to prepare |

⛔ **Putting 18,987 into the wages field answers ② with "yes, it was payroll" while line 8 answers "no".**
**Artem's K-1 now tells his 1040 preparer he received 18,987 of W-2 wages, and the same return says the
company paid no salaries.** 🛠️ **A preparer who believes the K-1 will look for a W-2 that does not exist.**

✅ **AND THE MASCIAVE MODEL DOES NOT PUT IT THERE EITHER.** Alejandra's officer compensation is **74,000**
and it is **100% hers** — yet her K-1's §199A W-2 wages is **not 74,000**; it is a different figure
altogether *(§8C ②)*. ⛔ **So even on the return being copied, the officer compensation does not go in
that field.**

🔑 **THE REASSURANCE THAT MATTERS, because it is the natural worry: with the field at ZERO, nothing on
the 1120-S says whose the compensation is — AND THAT IS EXACTLY RIGHT.** Form 1125-E is not filed at
these receipts, so the company return never states the split, and it does not need to. **The attribution
is made by putting the amount on ARTEM's Schedule C and not on Ishkhan's** — which is precisely how
Masciave does it. ✅ **Zeroing the field does not lose the decision; it stops the return contradicting
itself.**

⛔ ~~**THE FIX IS UNCHANGED: `Section 199A W-2 wages` = 0, on BOTH.**~~ 🛑 **SUPERSEDED BY §4 DECISION 28 —
THE FIELD STAYS AT 18,987, ALL ON ARTEM, WHICH IS WHAT THE FILED RETURN CARRIES.** ⛔ **Do not zero it.**

#### 🎯 THE 13,005 IS SOLVED — and it was one field all along

📄 **Page 13 of the generated return, statement `Line 17d, Sch K (1120S) — Other Items and Amounts`:**

> **`AC  Code AC - Gross receipts for section 448(c) ....... AC   26,010`**

🔑 **26,010 ÷ 2 = 13,005.** ⛔ **ATX was never printing an entity figure on both K-1s. It was printing the
HALF of an entity figure — and doing that correctly.** The only thing wrong is the entity input value.

🟢 **THE SENTENCE BELOW WAS RIGHT.** *"The fix is one field, not two: change the entity §448(c)
gross-receipts figure from `26,010` to `269,901`, and ATX will put 134,950 / 134,951 on the two K-1s by
itself."*
🛑 **On 2026-09-05 a session struck it through and replaced it with "code AC reports the average of the
three preceding years". THAT replacement was reading 3, and it is dead** — §3T-4's top box. **The strike
has been removed; nothing in this paragraph is superseded except the claim that it was.**
📌 **WHAT WAS ACTUALLY FILED IS NEITHER: §4 decision 29 — ROUTE A**, the rolled-over figure left on both
K-1s at **13,005**, with only the manual override removed. ⚖️ **Route B — the 269,901 above — is the
technically correct figure and remains Julia's to elect.**

🛑 **AND THE CORRECTION I OWE MYSELF, because I told Lilian two different things and both were half
right.** I first said *"the field is entity-level — put 269,901 in it, not a half."* She asked whether it
should not be the half, since box 17 lives on each K-1. I then said she was right and I was wrong.
🔑 **The truth is that BOTH were right about different things:** the **input** is entity-level and takes
the full **269,901**; the **output** on each K-1 is the **half**. ⛔ **My error was reading the repeated
13,005 as evidence of an entity figure pushed to both K-1s — when it was the half of 26,010, and the
software was behaving correctly the whole time.** 📌 **The reusable lesson: when a figure appears
identically on two K-1s, look for the entity statement that feeds them before concluding anything about
the field.**

### 3T-4 · 🎯 **CODE AC — SETTLED 2026-09-06. The answer is 269,901, and Lilian was right twice**

> 🔵 **DECIDED 2026-09-06 — §4 decision 29: ROUTE A. The 2025 return is FILED with the software's
> rolled-over figure on BOTH K-1s — `13,005` each — and the only change made is REMOVING the manual
> `134,951` override from Artem's K-1.** ⚖️ **Lilian's call, provisional pending Julia's review**, taken
> once it was established that the box feeds nobody's tax. **The paragraph below is what route B would
> have been, and it is the technically correct figure — kept because Julia may still choose it.**
>
> 🟢 **THE ANSWER, before the history: the entity figure is the CURRENT YEAR's gross receipts —
> `269,901` — and each K-1 carries the shareholder's 50% share: `134,951` / `134,950`.**
> ⛔ **NOT the three-prior-year average.** ⛔ **NOT 26,010.** 🔑 **And filling the 2022 and 2023 rows of
> ATX's §448 grid does NOTHING to this figure** — Lilian challenged that premise twice and was right
> both times.
>
> 🛑 **EVERYTHING BELOW THIS BOX IS THE HISTORY OF FOUR WRONG READINGS AND HOW EACH WAS KILLED.**
> **A session that needs the figure has it. Read on only to avoid re-deriving the error.**

#### 🧪 WHAT SETTLED IT — two pieces of evidence, one of them Lilian's own test

**① THE SHAREHOLDER'S WORKSHEET WANTS ONE YEAR AT A TIME, NOT AN AVERAGE.** 📄 **Instructions for
Form 8990 (Rev. 12-2025), the `Average Annual Gross Receipts Worksheet Per Section 448(c)`:**

| Line | | **Column A** | **Column B** | **Column C** |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| | | *1st preceding tax year* | *2nd preceding tax year* | *3rd preceding tax year* |
| **1** | Annual gross receipts | $ | $ | $ |
| **2** | Plus annual gross receipts of related entities per aggregate rules | $ | $ | $ |
| **3** | Total annual gross receipts | $ | $ | $ |
| **4** | **Average annual gross receipts** *(line 3, columns A + B + C, **divided by 3**)* | | | $ |

🔑 **The shareholder computes the average HIMSELF, at line 4, from three separate ANNUAL figures.**
⛔ **An average handed to him on one K-1 cannot be entered anywhere on that worksheet** — there is no
row for it. **What he needs from the S corporation is ONE YEAR's gross receipts: the year the K-1
covers.** ✅ **He builds the three columns from three consecutive K-1s.** 🔗 **And it is consistent with
the whole form: every other figure on a 2025 K-1 is a 2025 figure.**

**② LILIAN'S OWN TEST KILLED THE GRID THEORY.** On 2026-09-06 she **unchecked** *"Check to use Gross
Receipts Test worksheet to determine status as a Small Business Taxpayer"* on the `Line 13 (1120S) -
Interest Expense` worksheet — **and the `Code AC` box still read 26,010.** ✅ **That is branch 4 of the
four-outcome test below: the grid does NOT drive the field.** 🔑 **She also noticed the box was
unchecked on the 2024 return** — so it has never driven it.

| What the §448 grid actually is | What it is **not** |
|---|---|
| An **optional helper** for one thing only: **Schedule B question 10b** — ⛔ **NOT question 2, which asks for the business activity and product.** 📄 **Verbatim from the 2025 Form 1120-S:** question 10 asks *"Does the corporation satisfy one or more of the following?"* and 10b reads *"The corporation's aggregate average annual gross receipts (determined under section 448(c)) for the 3 tax years preceding the current tax year are more than $31 million **and the corporation has business interest expense**."* — which the screenshot shows answered **No**, correctly | ⛔ **the source of code AC**, ⛔ anything that touches a tax figure, ⛔ anything that needs 2022 or 2023 filled in |

🔑 **SO WHERE DID 26,010 COME FROM? It is 2024's gross receipts, ROLLED OVER and never updated.** The
`Code AC` box is a **manual input** that ATX carries forward each year — which is also why it accepts
typing, and why ATX cannot compute it: §448(c) can require aggregating **related entities' receipts**,
which the software has no way to know.

#### 🛠️ THE FIX — three steps, and one of them is undoing my advice

| # | Do this | Why |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | ⏸️ **NOT DONE — route A.** *(Route B would key `Ln 17d, Sch K - Oth Items` → `Code AC` = `269,901`: the entity's own 2025 gross receipts — page 1 line 1c **269,901**, lines 4 and 5 zero, no interest/dividend/rent/royalty/capital-gain items on Schedule K.)* | 📌 **one field, if Julia elects it** |
| **2** | ✅ **DO THIS EITHER WAY — remove the manual override from Artem's K-1** | under route A both K-1s fall back to **13,005**; under route B both compute to **134,951 / 134,950**. ⛔ **What cannot ship is 134,951 against 13,005** |
| **3** | 🗑️ **Leave the Gross Receipts Test checkbox UNCHECKED**, as 2024 did | it changes nothing on the return; Schedule B question 10 is answered directly and correctly. ⛔ **Do NOT fill the 2022/2023 rows for this purpose — they do not feed anything** |

#### ⚖️ THE FOUR READINGS THIS PAPER WENT THROUGH — recorded so nobody repeats them

| | Reading | Figure | How it died |
|---|---|---:|---|
| **1** | Entity 269,901 on **both** K-1s, un-halved | 269,901 ×2 | ⛔ **Lilian:** box 17 lives on each shareholder's own K-1, so it must be a share. 📄 Form 8990: *"in proportion to … the S corporation's shareholder's pro rata share of gross receipts."* **She was right** |
| **2** | The repeated 13,005 means ATX pushed an entity figure to both K-1s | — | ⛔ It was **half of 26,010**. The software was halving correctly all along |
| **3** | Code AC = the **three-prior-year average**, so fill 2022 and 2023 | 55,677 → 27,839 each | ⛔ **WRONG, and it was mine.** Built on one coincidence: with a single row filled, the grid's average EQUALS that row. 🔑 **Lilian asked "¿por qué crees que llenar 2022 y 2023 arregla esto?" — the honest answer was that it wouldn't**, and her unchecking test proved it |
| **4** | Leave 26,010, since the 2024 return also had the box unchecked | 26,010 | ⛔ **The checkbox was never the reason.** 26,010 is **2024's** gross receipts sitting in a rolled-over manual field — wrong under every reading of what code AC means |
| ✅ **5** | **Code AC = the CURRENT year, halved** | ✅ **269,901 → 134,951 / 134,950** | **The one that survives both tests above** |

📌 **THE REUSABLE LESSON, and it is not about §448(c).** 🛑 **A figure that matches is not a figure that
is caused.** Three times in two days a numeric coincidence was read as a mechanism — 13,005 as an
entity figure, 26,010 as an average, the grid as the source — and each time the correction came from
**Lilian challenging the premise**, not from re-checking the arithmetic. 🔑 **When a value appears in two
places, the next move is to CHANGE ONE AND SEE, not to explain the match.** *(Her unchecking test cost
one click and settled what three rounds of reasoning could not.)*

#### ⚖️ AND IT MOVES NO TAX — said once, so nobody treats this as a filing risk

The §448(c) threshold is **$31,000,000**. The company is at 269,901 and each shareholder at 134,951.
**Form 8990 is not required for the entity** *(Schedule B question 10 answered No — correctly)*, §163(j)
does not bite on anyone, and **no figure on this return or on either 1040 changes.** 🔑 **It is a
disclosure that has to be right, not a computation that decides anything.**

#### 📜 HISTORY — the route Lilian found, and the reasoning that went wrong on the way

> ⛔ **DO NOT ACT ON ANYTHING FROM HERE TO THE END OF §3T-4.** Everything below is superseded by the box
> at the top of this section and by **§4 decision 29**. 🛑 **It contains step-by-step instructions to do
> things this paper now forbids** — filling the 2022/2023 rows, keying an average, keying a figure on
> each K-1. **It is kept because the reasoning is seductive and someone will rebuild it, not because any
> of it is to be done.**

**Lilian found the route herself, 2026-09-05**, after telling me several times that she could not type in
line 17d. 🛑 **She was right every time, and the reason I kept missing it is worth recording: I was
describing WHAT to change and never WHERE it is entered.** Line 17d has **no dollar box on the form** —
the amount lives in a worksheet behind it, and the worksheet behind *that* is where the figure is really
made.

#### 🗺️ THE ROUTE — two screens, and only the second one is the source

| Step | Where | What is there |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | **Schedule K, page 4, line 17d** | 🔒 **greyed — it cannot be typed.** This is the printed output, nothing else |
| **2** | **Click the greyed line anyway** | ATX jumps to the worksheet **`Ln 17d, Sch K - Oth Items`**, where **`Code AC - Gross receipts for section 448(c)`** sits in an **editable** box carrying **`26010`**. ⚠️ **This is still an OUTPUT.** Typing over it works — and makes the figure a manual override that no longer agrees with anything |
| **3** | 🔑 **The likely source: the worksheet `Line 13 (1120S) - Interest Expense`** | It contains the **`IRC Sec. 448 Gross Receipts Test`** grid — a row per year, a `Short Yr Months` column, and an **`Average Gross Receipts`** line. ⚠️ **That this average FEEDS code AC is an INFERENCE from a numeric coincidence, not something observed — read the next block before acting on it** |

**What the grid holds today** *(read off Lilian's screenshot, 2026-09-05)*:

| Year | Begin | End | Gross receipts | | Total |
|---|---|---|---:|---|---:|
| 2022 | *(blank)* | *(blank)* | 🔴 **empty** | 0 | 0 |
| 2023 | *(blank)* | *(blank)* | 🔴 **empty** | 0 | 0 |
| 2024 | 1/1/2024 | 12/31/2024 | **26,010** | 0 | 26,010 |
| | | | **Average Gross Receipts** | | ✅ **26,010** |
| *2025 — current year* | 1/1/2025 | 12/31/2025 | *269,901* | 0 | *269,901* |

🔑 **26,010 ÷ 2 = 13,005**, which is the figure that appeared on both K-1s and matched nothing on the
return. **The software was correct end to end.** ⛔ **Two of the three input rows are empty.**

#### 🔬 WHAT IS OBSERVED vs WHAT IS INFERRED — **and the one keystroke that decides it**

**Lilian asked the right question — *"¿por qué crees que llenar 2022 y 2023 arregla el 26,010?"* — and it
exposes that the causal chain above is assumed, not seen.**

| ✅ **Observed** *(from the two screenshots)* | ❓ **Inferred** |
|---|---|
| `26,010` in the grid's **2024 row** | 🔑 **that the grid's average is what FEEDS the `Code AC` box** |
| `26,010` on the grid's **`Average Gross Receipts`** line | |
| `26,010` in the **`Code AC`** box of the 17d worksheet | |
| `13,005` = 26,010 ÷ 2 on **both K-1s** | |

🛑 **The logical hole: with ONE row populated, the average IS that row.** So the 26,010 in the AC box is
equally consistent with three different origins, and they look identical from outside:

| | Where it would come from | Does filling 2023/2022 fix it? |
|---|---|---|
| **(a)** | the grid's **average** | ✅ **yes** |
| **(b)** | the **2024 row alone** *(the prior year, not an average)* | ⛔ **no** |
| **(c)** | a value **rolled over from the 2024 ATX file** or typed once, that merely **coincides** | ⛔ **no** |

⚠️ **AND THIS PAPER ALREADY CONCLUDED (c), IN §F2:** *"ATX is not COMPUTING this figure from the return —
it is carrying it from an INPUT, either typed into the §448(c)/§163(j) gross-receipts field or rolled
over from the prior-year ATX file. That is why Lilian can overtype it."* ⛔ **The §448 grid was then
found, the numbers matched, and the earlier conclusion was never revisited. Matching is not causing.**

| Points to **(a)** | Points to **(c)** |
|---|---|
| **2022 and 2023 being empty fits a grid maintained by hand that rolls forward ONE year at a time** — the 2024 file carried 26,010 as its current year, and rolling to 2025 dropped it into the 2024 row. Nobody has ever filled the other two | **The `Code AC` box accepts typing.** In ATX a purely computed field is normally protected — as line 17d on Schedule K itself is |

##### 🧪 THE TEST — one keystroke, and it settles which one it is

**Type `141,021` into the 2023 row and watch TWO things, in this order:**

| What happens | What it means | What to do |
|---|---|---|
| `Average Gross Receipts` changes **and** `Code AC` follows **by itself** | ✅ **(a) confirmed** — the grid is the source | carry on: settle 2022 and it is done |
| `Average` changes but **`Code AC` stays 26,010** | the box holds a **manual or rolled-over value**, unlinked or overridden | **clear the box** and see whether it repopulates with the average |
| `Average` does **not** change | the grid probably needs the row's **dates** too *(the 2024 row has them; the empty rows do not)* | enter `1/1/2023` and `12/31/2023`, look again |
| **nothing changes anywhere** | ⛔ **(b) or (c)** — the grid does not drive that field | **type the correct average straight into `Code AC`** |

⛔ ~~*"THE DESTINATION IS THE SAME IN ALL FOUR BRANCHES: the `Code AC` box must end up showing the
correct three-prior-year average."*~~ 🛑 **DEAD — there is no three-prior-year average in this box at
all.** ✅ **The test itself was sound and it is what settled the question:** Lilian ran it, landed in
branch 4 *(nothing changed anywhere)*, and that is how the grid was ruled out. **The test's CONCLUSION
was right; its stated destination was wrong.**

#### 🛑 ~~THE THIRD CORRECTION: 269,901 DOES NOT BELONG IN CODE AC~~ — ⛔ **THIS IS READING 3, AND IT IS WRONG**

> ⛔ **DO NOT ACT ON THIS BLOCK.** It is the reasoning that produced 55,677 / 27,839, killed by the box
> at the top of §3T-4. **269,901 IS the figure.** Kept because the reasoning is seductive and someone
> will rebuild it: the trap is that the *entity's* §448(c) SMALL-BUSINESS TEST is a three-year average,
> while the *K-1 disclosure* is one year's receipts — **two different things wearing the same words.**

**Twice I told Lilian to put 269,901 in that field.** *(§F2's "what to put there" block, and again in
§3T-3.)* ⛔ **Both were wrong.** **269,901 is the CURRENT year.** Code AC reports the **average of the
three PRECEDING** tax years — which is exactly what the grid is built to compute, and why the 2025 row
sits *below* the average line rather than in it.

📄 **The Instructions for Form 1120-S (2025), pulled from irs.gov this session, state the test in terms:**

> *"A taxpayer meets the gross receipts test if the taxpayer has **average annual gross receipts of $31
> million or less for the 3 prior tax years**. A taxpayer's average annual gross receipts for the 3
> prior tax years is determined by **adding the gross receipts for the 3 prior tax years and dividing
> the total by 3**."*

🔑 **So on a 2025 return the three prior years are 2022, 2023 and 2024.** The 2025 row exists so that the
same worksheet answers the question again next year; it is not this year's disclosure.

#### ✅ 2024's **26,010 IS CORRECT** — I called it suspicious, and it is not

📖 **The filed 2024 return, read 2026-09-04** (§E): page 1 **line 1a = 26,010**, cost of goods sold
9,181, gross profit 16,829 — the first of the eight arithmetic checks §E's header lists, so the figure
is corroborated, not merely extracted. ⛔ **It looked wrong only beside 2025's 269,901.** ✅ **2024 really
did collapse:** §E-8d has the three years side by side — **2023: 141,021 · 2024: 26,010 · 2025: 271,841
accrual (269,901 cash)**. **Nothing to fix in that row.**

#### 🛑 ~~THE REAL DEFECT: 2023 IS EMPTY AND SHOULD BE 141,021~~ — ⛔ **DEAD. NOTHING IS TYPED IN THAT ROW**

⛔ ~~*"The filed 2023 return, read 2026-09-02 on Lilian's written authorisation (§E-8, §E-8d). §448(c)
gross receipts are NOT reduced by cost of goods sold, so the figure to type is the 141,021, never the
49,936 of gross profit."*~~ 🛑 **The 2023 row feeds nothing** — the grid does not drive code AC. **There
is no "real defect" here and no figure to type.**
✅ **The ONE sentence worth keeping, because it is a general rule and true anywhere it applies:**
**§448(c) gross receipts are NOT reduced by cost of goods sold** — it is in the SOP at
[§11F ④](../../sops/form-1120s-preparation.md).

#### 🟡 ~~2022 IS THE ONLY GENUINE UNKNOWN — and it decides the DIVISOR~~ — ⛔ **MOOT for code AC**

> ⛔ **2022 and 2023 do not affect code AC at all** *(Lilian's unchecking test, top of §3T-4)*. 🟢 **But the
> `(3,129)` finding below is INDEPENDENT of §448(c) and it stands** — it is evidence about when the S
> election began and about the C-corporation question in §3S row 3. **Read it for that, not for this.**

The company was incorporated **6/22/2022**, so its first tax year, if it had one, was a **short year**.
Two things nobody has established:

1. ~~**Does a 2022 federal return exist for this company?** Never looked for.~~ 🟢 **ANSWERED FROM
   EVIDENCE ALREADY IN THIS PAPER, 2026-09-06 — IT ALMOST CERTAINLY DOES. See the block below.**

   **and how many months the short period ran.**

##### 🔎 ~~2022 ALMOST CERTAINLY EXISTS~~ — 🟢 **MOVED OUT OF THIS BANNED BLOCK**

🔗 **The `(3,129)` evidence is NOT code-AC history and is NOT superseded — it has been lifted into its
own live section, [§E-9](#e-9), because §3S row 3, §3S row 10 and §CT-FIX CT4 all cite it.** ⛔ **Nothing
about it depends on anything in §3T-4.**

📄 **The annualisation rule, verbatim from the Instructions for Form 8990 (Rev. 12-2025):**

> *"If your business was **not in existence for the entire 3-year period**, base your average annual
> gross receipts on **the period your business existed**. Also, if your business had a **tax year of less
> than 12 months**, your gross receipts must be **annualized by multiplying the gross receipts for the
> short period by 12 and dividing the result by the number of months in the short period**. The prior
> period gross receipts must be annualized for any short period **before dividing by 3**."*

🔑 **That is precisely what ATX's `Short Yr Months` column is for** — type the months, and it annualises
before averaging.

#### 🧮 ~~WHAT THE FIGURE COMES OUT AT — the three cases~~ — ⛔ **ALL THREE ARE MOOT**

> ⛔ **None of these is the answer.** The average was never the disclosure. ✅ **The one line here that
> SURVIVES is the pro-rata rule** — each K-1 carries the shareholder's **50% share** — which is what makes
> the answer **134,951 / 134,950**, not 269,901 twice.

**🔑 FIRST, THE HALF IS NOT IN DOUBT — only the figure being halved is.** 📄 **Instructions for Form
8990 (Rev. 12-2025):** *"a taxpayer with an ownership interest in a partnership or S corporation must
include a share of the partnership's or S corporation's gross receipts, in proportion to … the **S
corporation's shareholder's pro rata share of gross receipts**."* ✅ **Both shareholders are at 50%**
(§4 decision 21), so **each K-1 carries half of the entity figure** — which is exactly what ATX was
already doing when it printed 13,005 against an entity 26,010.

| | The 2022 situation | What to put in the 2022 row | 🔴 **Code AC — entity** | **Ishkhan's K-1** | **Artem's K-1** |
|---|---|---|---:|---:|---:|
| **ⓐ** | 🟡 **No 2022 return** — the first tax year was 2023. ⛔ **The UNLIKELY branch** — the (3,129) opening AAA says 2022 was a tax year | leave it **EMPTY** | (141,021 + 26,010) ÷ **2** = **83,516** | **41,758** | **41,758** |
| **ⓑ** | 🟢 **A 2022 short year with NO revenue** *(the most likely shape, unproven)* | **0** — ⓘ **and then `Short Yr Months` is irrelevant, because 0 annualised is still 0** | (0 + 141,021 + 26,010) ÷ **3** = **55,677** | **27,839** | **27,838** |
| **ⓒ** | A 2022 short year **with** revenue `G` over `m` months | **G**, and `Short Yr Months` = **`m`** | (G × 12 ÷ m + 167,031) ÷ **3** | half | half |

ⓘ **On `Short Yr Months` in case ⓒ:** the period runs **6/22/2022 → 12/31/2022**, a little over six
months. The customary count treats the partial month as a month, giving **7** — ⚠️ **a convention, not a
figure read off a form.** It only matters if 2022 actually had receipts.

ⓘ **On the odd dollar:** 55,677 ÷ 2 = 27,838.50, so ATX will print **27,839 / 27,838** or **27,839 on
both**. 🛑 **Read what it prints and leave it — do not force the split.** At a threshold of 31,000,000 a
dollar decides nothing.

⚠️ **ONE CAVEAT THAT IS NOT NEW AND IS NOT SPECIFIC TO THIS FIGURE.** The 50/50 assumes both men were
shareholders **for the whole year at 50%**. 🔴 **If Artem was admitted DURING 2025, §1377(a)(1) requires a
per-day allocation and no pro-rata item on either K-1 is a flat half** — box 1 and box 16D first among
them. **That is §3S row 8 and it is already open**; code AC simply inherits it. ⓘ *Unlike boxes 1 and
16D, here it changes nothing that matters — both figures are three orders of magnitude below the
threshold.*

⚠️ **Verify the `Average Gross Receipts` line after typing, do not assume it.** Today ATX divides by the
number of rows carrying a figure — one row, average 26,010 — **which happens to match the rule here**,
but "happens to match" is not the same as "implements it".

#### 🛠️ ~~THE FIX, IN ORDER~~ — ⛔ **SUPERSEDED by the three-step fix at the top of §3T-4**

| # | Do this | How you know it worked |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | `Line 13 (1120S) - Interest Expense` → the **§448 grid** → **2023 row: begin `1/1/2023`, end `12/31/2023`, gross receipts `141,021`** | the row totals 141,021 |
| **2** | **The 2022 row — only once question 1 above is answered.** Case ⓐ: leave it empty | — |
| **3** | Read the **`Average Gross Receipts`** line | it recomputes to **83,516** (case ⓐ) or **55,677** (case ⓑ) |
| **4** | Back to **`Ln 17d, Sch K - Oth Items`** | 🔑 **the `Code AC` box should now show the new average BY ITSELF.** ⛔ **If it still reads 26,010 it is holding an override — clear the field so it computes** |
| **5** | 🔴 **REMOVE the `134,951` typed by hand on Artem's K-1** | it is **half of 269,901** — the current year, the figure this section supersedes — and it will disagree with everything above |
| **6** | Re-generate and read both K-1s | ✅ **both carry the SAME half**, and it is the half of the average |

#### ⚖️ HOW MUCH THIS ACTUALLY MATTERS — said plainly, because it is easy to over-weight

🔑 **No tax moves — not on this return, not on either 1040.** The §448(c) threshold is **$31,000,000**;
every candidate figure above is under **one third of one percent** of it. The company is a small-business
taxpayer either way, **Form 8990 is not filed either way**, §163(j) does not bite either way, and each
shareholder passes his own test either way. ⛔ **That is a reason to fix it calmly, not a reason to skip
it** — it is a figure on a signed return and on two people's K-1s, and the only thing wrong with it is
that two rows of a worksheet were never filled in.

### E-9 · 🟢 **2022 WAS ALMOST CERTAINLY A TAX YEAR — the evidence, lifted out of §3T-4** <a id="e-9"></a>

🛑 **This is NOT part of the code-AC history and none of §3T-4's do-not-act banner reaches it.** It was
found while chasing the §448(c) grid, it turned out to have nothing to do with it, and **it BEARS on three live
sections** — §3S row 3 *(the C-corporation question)*, §3S row 10 and **§CT-FIX CT4** *(whether Connecticut
returns exist for the earlier years)*. ⚠️ **“Bears on”, not “is cited by”:** §3S row 3 has never mentioned
it, and the other two restate the evidence rather than linking here. **Read this section before relying on
any of them.**

🔑 **The 2023 return's Schedule M-2 opens at `(3,129)`**, and the schedule is internally exact
*(−3,129 + 300 − 11,034 − 347 = −14,210, matching its own Schedule L)*. **An AAA does not exist outside an
S year, and it starts at ZERO in the first one.** ⛔ **So a NON-ZERO deficit at 1/1/2023 can only have
been produced by a preceding S tax year — 2022 — in which the company operated at a loss of 3,129.**

| What that settles | |
|---|---|
| ✅ **2022 was a tax year, and an S year** | so **“there is simply no 2022 return” is the unlikely branch, not the safe default** |
| ✅ **The S election predates 2023, not just 2024** | §E-8d already had it predating 2024; this pushes it back one more year |
| ✅ **More evidence against a C-corporation history** | a C year would have left AAA at zero on the first S year, not at (3,129). 🔗 **It bears on §3S row 3 and on the accumulated-E&P premise in §E-2 ④** — recorded there, not re-argued here |
| ⛔ **What it does NOT settle** | **2022's gross receipts.** A 3,129 loss is consistent with a start-up stub that billed nothing **and** with one that billed and spent more. 🛑 **Do not infer zero revenue from a small loss** |

🛠️ **What it means in practice: the question about 2022 is not "does a return exist?" but "open it and
read line 1a"** — ⓘ *the firm prepared 2024 (§E-7, signed by Yuliia Kononova), so ATX is the natural place
to look; whether the firm prepared 2022 is not established.* ⛔ **And for code AC it does not matter at
all** — that is §3T-4's whole conclusion.

them.** §3F–§3Q are deliberately built at **depreciation = 0** and **§3H-2 restates every figure that
moves** *(the banner at the head of §3 says why)*. **A check that confirms 40,345.18 confirms the wrong
number.** ✅ **The four affected rows carry both columns below**, and §3H-2 re-runs the same three
checks at D = 26,700 — they all still hold.

### B · 🔴 ONE shareholder in 2025 — and on Julia's date it is ISHKHAN. What is left is **his basis, and WHOSE money the 2025 equity movements are**

##### 🔴 B-0 · JULIA'S DATE, 2026-09-02 (evening) — ISHKHAN IS THE 2025 SHAREHOLDER. What changes, what survives

**Julia to Lilian, relayed the same evening:** *"la fecha en la que Ishkhan dejó la compañía fue al final del

only half true** *(sharpened after review)*: the transcript also said **FY2025 carries two K-1s**, and it does
not — Julia's answer is a **third** option neither the transcript nor Lilian's date stated. **The one-year
contradiction is closed on the date; nobody should treat the transcript as vindicated wholesale.**

| | Was (fifth and sixth pass) | Now |
|---|---|---|
| 2025 shareholder | Artem, 100% | **Ishkhan Markarian, 100%, 365/365 days** — the same shareholder as 2023 and 2024 |
| K-1 | one, to Artem | **one, to ISHKHAN** — his third consecutive K-1 from this company and his **final** one. 🛠️ **Two entry points nobody should guess at** *(flagged in review)*: the **"Final K-1"** box — the only instruction the IRS gives ties it to the *corporation's* final return, so checking it for a departing shareholder is ordinary practice rather than a printed rule, **and it is what tells his 1040 no further K-1 is coming**; and 🔴 **Item H, "number of shares", beginning and end of year** — **100 at the beginning, and the end-of-year figure is the one to get right**, because the software allocates from it and a `0` can produce a weighted-average percentage instead of the 100% this return needs. **Check the computed percentage on the printed K-1 before filing** |
| §1377 one-day question | assumed away | **does not arise** — the transfer is at the close of the last day of the year, so he holds stock on all 365 days, §1377(a)(1)'s per-day allocation collapses to 100%, and Artem's first day is 2026-01-01. *(A §1377(a)(2) closing-of-the-books election is neither available nor needed.)* ⚠️ *The reciprocal proposition this paper cites elsewhere — that Reg. §1.1377-1(a)(2)(ii) treats a transferor as owning the stock on the day of disposition — **could not be verified from the regulation text in this environment** and is not relied on here* |
| Opening basis | Artem's — "created by the transfer", unknown | **Ishkhan's — it EXISTS: line 15 of his 2024 Form 7203**, filed with his own 2024 Form 1040. ⛔ **Another client's document — not opened here.** *(Double holds him as "Ishkhan Markarian & Oksana Markaryan", cid 710638, a joint individual client; whether the firm prepared that 1040 was not read in this pass)* |
| Suspended losses from 2024 | Ishkhan's, dead on transfer | 🟡 **IN PLAY, but far less than a profit year suggests — the ORDER is against it.** 🛑 **CORRECTED 2026-09-02 after review: DISTRIBUTIONS COME BEFORE LOSSES.** Form 7203's instructions set the annual order — ① increases for income and contributions · ② **decrease for distributions (box 16D)** · ③ decrease for nondeductible expenses · ④ **decrease for losses and deductions** *(Reg. §1.1367-1(f))*. So the 2025 income does **not** free the loss first: the loss reaches only whatever basis **survives the distributions** 🛑 *(the two figures left in this sentence are PRE-JE 2025-ADJ 3 and are superseded — the current ones are **119,064.03** of distributions, **21,358.55** of room at step 3 and **140,422.58** before them: §3P)*, which under reading ① of §B-1 is *opening + 16,227.41* before the nondeductible items — **not** *opening + 131,950.36*. **Whatever is still suspended at the close of 2025-12-31 dies with the transfer.** ⓘ *The one reversal available, the Reg. §1.1367-1(g) election, moves losses ahead of NONDEDUCTIBLE items only — never ahead of distributions — and needs a statement with a timely filed return* |

The Form 8995 instructions say losses **"suspended by other Code provisions are not qualified losses and must be tracked separately for use when subsequently allowed"** — so **either** the 2024 loss was allowed *(a real QBI carryforward exists, and little or nothing was suspended for basis)* **or** it was suspended under §1366(d) *(no 2024 QBI carryforward yet)*. 🟢 **HALF-ANSWERED 2026-09-05 — THE 2024 FORM 7203 IS IN HAND** *(§3P-2)*: **it was BOTH.** Of the 86,905 of 2024 losses, **38,780 was allowed and 48,125 was suspended under §1366(d)** — so the (68,058)/(18,847) pair this paper carries as a §199A carryforward is the **gross** loss and is **probably overstated** as a QBI figure. 🛠️ **His 2024 Form 8995 is now the only missing half — ask for it with the same request that produced the 7203** |

"something else" is Julia's call, not a preparer's.

| **Redemption + new issue** *(the company buys Ishkhan out, then issues shares to Artem)* | a **§302 redemption**: sale-or-exchange treatment only as a complete termination (§302(b)(3)) **with a §302(c)(2) waiver of family attribution**, because his son is the remaining shareholder. 🛑 **The waiver has conditions this client may not meet, and they belong here:** (i) **no interest afterwards other than as a creditor — expressly including as officer, director or employee**; (ii) no re-acquisition for ten years except by bequest; (iii) **the agreement filed with the return** (Reg. §1.302-4(a)). ⚠️ **He remains the company's QuickBooks primary admin and signed the 2025 engagement letter in his own name** *(client file §3, §5)* — that is the (i) question, not an answer. **If the waiver fails it is a §1368 distribution — NOT a §301 dividend, because there is no accumulated E&P** (§E-2 ④): basis first, then capital gain | what he paid for the new shares | 🔴 **this one DOES touch the company's return** — cash out of the company, Schedule L moves, and **the AAA is reduced ratably by the redeemed percentage, "whether negative or positive" (§1368(e)(1)(B)) — so a 100% redemption would ELIMINATE the (89,293) deficit**, which §7's carryforward table does not reflect |

  | **It was a gift** from Ishkhan | 🔑 **Ishkhan's basis carries over** (§1015) — his 2024 Form 7203 becomes the source after all | ⚠️ **§1015(a)'s DUAL-BASIS rule bites here**: where the donor's basis exceeds fair value at the date of gift, the donee's basis **for determining LOSS** is fair value. **This company has negative equity and three loss/deficit years — that is a live branch, not a footnote** · the holding period **tacks** (§1223(2)) · and a gift may raise a **Form 709** for Ishkhan |

  suspended losses are PERSONAL to the shareholder and do not pass to a transferee.** If he had 2024
  losses suspended for want of basis and disposed of his entire interest, **they are permanently
  disallowed.** ⛔ **Artem inherits none of them.**

  company issue them?** ⚠️ **His basis cannot be computed until it is answered — but it does NOT decide
  whether there is a gain.** ✅ **The arithmetic below settles that on its own**, because basis can never
  be negative. **The figure is still needed for Form 7203 and for the 2026 opening balance.**

- 🔴 **THE EXPOSURE IS A BASIS QUESTION, NOT AN AAA QUESTION — and an earlier version of this paper had
  it backwards.** With **no accumulated E&P**, **§1368(b) governs and AAA plays no part in taxability at
  all**: the **whole** 115,722.95 reduces Artem's stock basis (§1368(b)(1)), and **any part exceeding his
  basis is capital gain to him** (§1368(b)(2)). ⛔ **Not "the excess over AAA".** AAA becomes the
  operative test only under **§1368(c)**, which needs accumulated E&P, and there is none.
  🛑 **The SOP names this error by name** ([`form-1120s-preparation.md`](../../sops/form-1120s-preparation.md)
  §10.0b): *"The AAA decides PRESENTATION. Basis decides TAX… getting these two backwards is the classic
  error."*
  🔑 **And now that there is only ONE shareholder, the arithmetic is simple — and it is far better news
  than this paper has been saying.** Basis moves in a fixed order (§1367/§1368): opening, **plus**
  contributions and income, **less** distributions, **less** non-deductible items and losses.

  | | |
  |---|---:|
  | Artem's opening basis at 2025-01-01 | **unknown — but it CANNOT be negative** |
  | plus 2025 contributions | **100,077.40** |
  | plus 2025 income — 🔴 **CASH, the return's basis** *(§4 decision 13; accrual would be 31,872.96)* | **29,932.96** |
  | less 2025 distributions | **(115,722.95)** |
  | **= ending basis, taking opening at its WORST CASE of zero** | **14,287.41** *(16,227.41 on the accrual figure)* |
  | less the meals 50% disallowance if the whole `Meals` balance is hit *(a non-deductible item)* | (7,071.14) |
  | **= still positive** | **7,216.27** *(9,156.27 on the accrual figure)* |

  🛑 **CORRECTION, and it matters because it changes what to worry about in the next thirteen days.**
  Earlier passes of this paper called the distributions *"the largest single exposure on the return."*
  **On the actual figures that is WRONG.** Basis can never be negative, so **the worst case for Artem's
  opening basis is zero — and even then he ends 2025 with basis left and there is NO capital gain.**
  ✅ **The distributions do not appear to cost him anything.**

  ⚠️ **Four things that could still move it, and the first is the one to check:** 🔴 **is any part of the
  100,077.40 actually a LOAN to the company booked into equity?** The SOP warns about exactly this
  (§5C-v), and Form 7203 line 2's own instruction is *"Don't include any loans to the S corporation"* —
  **a loan is DEBT basis, not stock basis**, and it would shrink or invert the 16,227.41 cushion. Then:
  the **tax** income will differ from the book 31,872.96; the **meals decision** and any other
  non-deductible item come off basis; and **if 2025 turns out to be a loss year after adjustments**,
  losses come off after distributions and could be suspended. 🔑 **And the reclassification decisions are roughly NEUTRAL to basis** — moving a
  cost from expense to distribution raises income by the same amount it raises distributions, so the two
  cancel.

  ⛔ **The basis figure is STILL NEEDED** — Form 7203 is filed with his 1040 whenever there are
  distributions, and 2026 opens from wherever 2025 ends. **But it is a computation to complete, not a
  crisis to solve.**

- ⚠️ **What the NEGATIVE AAA actually does — presentation, and it is a trap.** AAA can go negative from
  losses but **cannot be driven negative by distributions: the excess simply never enters Schedule M-2**
  (SOP §10A). With opening AAA at **(89,293)** *(the filed figure — Lilian's decision of 2026-09-02, §4 decision 2; the arithmetically right (103,503) would only make it more negative)* and
  2025 book income of **29,932.96 on the cash basis the return uses** *(§4 decision 13)*, **M-2 line 6 computes
to roughly (59,360)** *(about (56,019) after the §D-8 entry)* — so **line 7 will report
  distributions at or near ZERO while Schedule K 16d and the K-1 box 16D carry the full 115,722.95
  gross.** 🛑 **A zero M-2 line 7 beside a gross 16d is NOT the netting presentation this paper ruled
  out. Say so on the return.**

- **Firm netting policy (§5C-v) does NOT apply, and it is settled on evidence.** Gate 1 fails
  (115,722.95 > 100,077.40). 🟡 **Gate 2 corroborated, not proved** — M-2 column (c) is blank at both
  ends of 2024 **and of 2023**, but the SOP's check is the **Schedule B C-corporation-history question**,

  is negative against material distributions. 🔴 **Gate 4 FAILS** — 2024 did **not** run contributions
  through Schedule M-2 line 3. ⓘ *2023's line 3 carries **300**, unattributed — ⛔ **nothing establishes it

  **2023's paid-in capital moved 28,658 outside M-2**, so on the evidence 2023 did not run contributions

- **§5C-viii applies squarely:** lines 7 and 8 are zero while distributions are material — **and it is

no fixed assets.**
